### Where the mechanism lives in the contract

Added after the cross-track consistency pass (F21) found that none of the above was reachable from the DAO interface: the policy was specified here and had no method to be implemented against, and no track owned the gap.

`multi-db-strategy.md` §3c now defines, on the composite: **`DeleteExpired(ctx, class, olderThan) (SweepResult, error)`** — one retention class per call, using that class's own clock column, cascading to credentials and writing a `deletion_log` row in the same transaction — and **`DeleteProfile(ctx, id, reason, externalRef)`** for subject requests, on the same code path. `SweepResult` carries rows examined, rows deleted and the age of the oldest surviving row, which is the health check below.

### The health check, which is a requirement rather than a suggestion

A deletion job that stops running produces no error, no alert and no visible symptom. The only evidence is data that should be absent and isn't. So the sweep emits, per class and per run: **rows examined, rows deleted, and the age of the oldest surviving row in that class.**

=== table rows ===
35:| `deletion_log` (the audit trail itself) | `deleted_at` — the entry's own timestamp | **Proposed, pending ruling — see basis** | **unknown — needs a legal ruling, and this cell is the point.** The log exists to evidence that a deletion happened, which is only useful for as long as someone may ask. That period is a legal question about limitation windows and regulator expectations, not an engineering one, and no engineering rationale can substitute for it. Note the trap: the log is PII-free by construction, so it is *not* itself subject to the storage-limitation clock the other rows answer to — which means nothing forces a window on it, and "keep it forever" is the path of least resistance rather than a decision | Hard delete of expired entries | Nothing — no FK, by design | unknown — needs product/legal ruling | **Proposed** |

Sep 12, 3:17 PM PDTPriya Nandakumar
**05 re-run closed.** Both regressions were mine, both in `pii-governance.md`, both fixed. The first was a stale summary — I'd written the §3c pointer *before* Oren's review changed the signatures, and never went back. So the governance note was advertising `reason` as a caller parameter that the contract now explicitly rejects, which is worse than saying nothing: a reader following the governance note would have implemented exactly the thing the contract forbids. The second was a conflation I made: I added a retention row for the `deletion_log` **table** when the pass had ruled on the audit-log **stream**. Different objects, different enforcement sites. The stream row now exists with 04's sink policy named as its site, and Theo's `observability.md:43` resolves both ways. The table is deliberately *not* a retention row, with the reason written in — it fails both halves of the test Dana set: nothing forces a window on it, and no method sweeps it. If a window is ever ruled, implementing it is a contract change, not a config value. I'd rather that cost be visible at the time of the ruling than discovered after. **One thing I flagged as a pattern rather than an incident.** This is the third time in this track a document has been left pointing at a dependency that moved — after F28 and the stale `S4` reference. Same cause every time: I update the authoritative document and not its summaries. That's a *different* finding from the earlier one, and worth keeping separate. The earlier one said reviews scoped to a document can't see relationships between documents. === DeleteExpired( ===
pii-governance.md:76:- **`DeleteExpired(ctx, class, olderThan, maxRows) (SweepResult, error)`** — one retention class per call, using that class's own clock column, cascading to credentials and writing a `deletion_log` row in the same transaction. **Batched: `maxRows` is required (1..10 000) and each batch is its own transaction**, so an interrupted sweep leaves fewer rows deleted rather than a half-written batch. The caller loops until `SweepResult.Drained` is true. It must not be called with a request-scoped context — a sweep cancelled because an inbound request went away is a retention policy silently dependent on request lifetimes.
multi-db-strategy.md:105:    DeleteExpired(ctx context.Context, class RetentionClass, olderThan time.Time, maxRows int) (SweepResult, error)
multi-db-strategy.md:272:**`DeleteExpired(ctx, class, olderThan, maxRows)`** — one class per call, never a mixed-class predicate, because a single predicate spanning two clocks is how the wrong window gets applied to the wrong rows.
backlog.md:127:**Description.** The cross-track consistency pass (F21) found that `pii-governance.md` specified a bulk retention sweep and a subject-deletion request sharing one code path, each writing a `deletion_log` row, and `multi-db-strategy.md` §5 defined the table — but **no method on the interface could reach any of it.** `Profiles().Delete` wrote no log row, and §3 forbids 03 from opening a transaction to combine the two, so a policy this track ruled on could not be implemented against the contract this track wrote, and no track owned the gap. Fixed by adding two composite-level methods in `multi-db-strategy.md` §3c: `DeleteExpired(ctx, class, olderThan) (SweepResult, error)` and `DeleteProfile(ctx, id, reason, externalRef)`, both writing `deletion_log` in the same transaction as the delete. `SweepResult` returns rows examined, rows deleted, and the age of the oldest surviving row — the last being the only signal that detects a silently stopped sweep, since rows-deleted cannot distinguish a broken sweep from an empty one.

pii-governance.md:77:- **`DeleteProfile(ctx, id, externalRef)`** for subject requests, on the same code path. **`reason` is not a parameter** — the method asserts `subject_request` internally, as `DeleteExpired` asserts `retention_sweep`. A caller-supplied reason code would let a copy-paste error mislabel an audit row, which destroys the one property the log exists to have: that its reasons are trustworthy.
multi-db-strategy.md:106:    DeleteProfile(ctx context.Context, id string, externalRef *string) error
multi-db-strategy.md:280:**`DeleteProfile(ctx, id, externalRef)`** — subject-deletion. Writes the `deletion_log` row in the same transaction, with **`reason` set internally to `subject_request`; it is not a parameter.**

## The interface contract (prose, not code)

> **Superseded — read the Go-shaped contract below, not this section.** This is the original first-wave prose, kept for the reasoning it carries and because the corrections in it are part of the record. Where it disagrees with "The Go-shaped contract" further down, **the contract wins**. Two specific claims below were overruled and are marked inline: `Update` is *not* an upsert, and phone search is exact-only. Found by this track's own seam-13 sweep (every restated signature grep-verified against its authoritative source), not by a reviewer — which is the point of making it a check rather than a review.

Three repository interfaces, one per entity, each implemented by two backend packages (below) and never containing SQL themselves — the service layer depends only on these shapes:

- **`ProfileRepository`** — `Get(ctx, id) (*UserProfile, error)`; `Search(ctx, query) (results []UserProfile, total int, error)` where `query` filters by name (partial/fuzzy), phone (~~exact/prefix~~ — **overruled: exact only, on the E.164-normalized value; ruled with 01 under consistency-pass finding F9**), region, country, plus limit/offset for pagination; `Create(ctx, *UserProfile) (*UserProfile, error)`; `Update(ctx, *UserProfile) (*UserProfile, error)` ~~implemented as a portable upsert~~ — **overruled: `Update` never creates and returns `ErrNotFound` on a missing row; the upsert form is the separate `Upsert` method (§3)**; `Delete(ctx, id) error`.
- **`CredentialRepository`** — `GetByUsername(ctx, username) (*UserCredential, error)` as the primary auth lookup path; `ListByUserID(ctx, userID) ([]UserCredential, error)`; `Create`, `Update`, `Delete` mirroring `ProfileRepository`'s shape. Never accepts or returns `UserProfile` fields.
- **`AuthMethodRepository`** — `Get(ctx, id)`, `GetByName(ctx, name)`, `List(ctx, activeOnly bool)`. Read-heavy; writes to this table are an operational action (adding a supported method), not part of the normal request path.

Domain types implied by the above: `UserProfile` (`ID`, `Name`, `Phone`, `StreetAddress`, `Locality`, `Region`, `PostalCode`, `Country`, `Source` — `"direct"` or `"idp_cache"` — `CreatedAt`, `UpdatedAt`); `UserCredential` (`ID`, `UserID` FK, `Username` unique, `MethodID` FK, `Secret` as bytes/nil, `HashAlgo`, `HashCost`, `CreatedAt`, `UpdatedAt`); `AuthMethod` (`ID`, `Name`, `RequiresSecret` bool, `IsActive` bool). A shared `ErrNotFound` sentinel is returned by every backend implementation for a missing row, so the service layer never has to distinguish `sql.ErrNoRows` from a SQLite-specific miss.

=== :225-230 ===
- `Upsert` — the portable `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT (id) DO UPDATE` form. Exists because the IDP hydration path genuinely does not know whether the row exists, which is a real caller rather than a hypothetical one. Any other caller should use `Create` or `Update`.

**`Update` and `Upsert` are FULL REPLACEMENTS, not partial patches.** This is the single most consequential thing in this document and it was missing from the first draft. Every column is written from the struct you pass. **A nil `Phone` sets the stored phone to NULL; it does not leave the existing value alone.** A caller wanting patch semantics must read, modify, and write back. Stated this loudly because both readings are reasonable and the wrong guess silently destroys data.

**`Upsert` conflicts on `id` only, and the caller must already hold that `id`.** It exists for re-hydrating a row already known to exist — not for resolving an unknown one. A cold read caught the circularity in the original justification: the IDP path "does not know whether the row exists" and therefore does not know its `id` either, so `Upsert` could not have served the caller it was justified with. **Resolving an `/identity` payload to an existing profile row is the connector's problem, not the DAO's** — there is deliberately no unique key on `phone` or `name` to match on, because neither is unique in reality. The connector decides identity and then calls `Create` or `Update`.

**`Delete` on a missing row returns `ErrNotFound`**, in both repositories — not a silent nil. Consistent with `Update`. Callers wanting idempotent delete check `errors.Is(err, ErrNotFound)` and treat it as success.

225:**`Upsert` conflicts on `id` only, and the caller must already hold that `id`.** It exists for re-hydrating a row already known to exist — not for resolving an unknown one. A cold read caught the circularity in the original justification: the IDP path "does not know whether the row exists" and therefore does not know its `id` either, so `Upsert` could not have served the caller it was justified with. **Resolving an `/identity` payload to an existing profile row is not the DAO's problem** — there is deliberately no unique key on `phone` or `name` to match on, because neither is unique in reality. It belongs to **`api-service`**, which is the party that both decides identity and holds a DAO handle: 03's layout gives `idp-connector` no database access at all (`../03-engineering-delivery/decisions/go-layout-debate.md`), so the connector fetches the payload and its caller decides what the payload *is* and then calls `Create` or `Update`. An earlier version of this sentence said "the connector" and was wrong about which process can reach the database (finding F22).

Renata's flag turned out to be a gap in my contract rather than just a risk of confusion on her side. **§7 named only the behavioural suite while its title implied both kinds of conformance.** So "conformance suite: done" could have been ticked by a signature diff — and the per-driver ruling is *explicitly conditional* on the behavioural suite existing. SQLite's `LIKE` case-insensitivity, collation ordering, `Country` normalization: each implementation correct when read alone, all three invisible without running the same assertions against both. The shape is worth naming, because it's now the third instance: **my document specified a requirement and named no mechanism precise enough to satisfy it unambiguously, and the gap only became visible when someone downstream had to act on it.** F21 was the same — a retention policy with no method to implement it. So was the registration-atomicity hole — a transaction bar with no atomic path supplied. Three times the contract has been wrong in a way no review *of the contract* could find, and each time the person implementing against it caught it. That's a different failure class from the five restatement drifts and from the between-documents contradictions. Those were about copies and relationships. This one is about the gap between specifying a requirement and specifying it precisely enough to be satisfiable — and the only reliable detector so far has been someone trying to build it. Two signed amendments in the log now, A1 and A2, both naming who found what. Holding, on call.

Checked, and it resolved itself between Desmond's look and mine — **nothing needed pushing.** `§1a`, the amendments log, and the A2 §7 split are all on `origin/main` (picked up by `e82fdc2`), and my working copy is byte-identical. `git diff origin/main -- 05-data-ops/` is empty. His read was correct on the evidence in front of him; the state moved underneath it. The shared tree's HEAD was several commits behind at `0cc3ff9`, so the file legitimately showed as modified relative to *that* — while `main` had already advanced past it. I created a worktree to do the push properly (per the shared-tree rule and the persona-attribution convention), found there was nothing to commit, and removed the worktree and branch rather than leave litter. Two things I passed back that outlast this particular false alarm: **`TestContractChecksumUpToDate` is seam 13 in executable form** — a restatement (the checksum) of an authoritative source (the contract) that fails loudly on divergence. It's the strongest version of what this track has been arguing for all along, because unlike a review it can't be skipped by someone who reasonably assumes the doc is current. I'd rather it fire spuriously than not exist. **And the sharper instrument for next time:** a "file modified" signal in the shared tree is relative to *that tree's HEAD*, not to `origin/main`. With this many sessions committing concurrently, those diverge routinely. The question "is this doc pushed?" is answered by `git diff origin/main -- <path>` after a fetch, not by `git status`. That's not a correction to his conclusion — he flagged a real divergence and recommended the right remedy — just a better tool for a repo with a dozen concurrent writers. Working tree clean, no stray worktrees, contract current on main. Holding, on call.

{
  "file_path": "/Users/warrenthompson/Source/LoginID/05-data-ops/ddl-review-checklist.md",
  "content": "# DDL review checklist — what 05 checks before a migration PR merges\n\nOwner: 05-data-ops (Priya Nandakumar). Audience: **track 03, before writing migrations** — not only 05 at review time. Published ahead of LT-36/LT-37 so the checks are known in advance rather than discovered in review; the same reasoning as amendment A1, where flagging a contract ambiguity before implementation cost one message and finding it at review would have cost a rewrite.\n\n**This introduces no new decisions.** Every item derives from `./multi-db-strategy.md` (the contract), `./migration-approach.md`, or `./pii-governance.md`, and each line names its source. If an item here disagrees with the contract, the contract wins and I have made an error — tell me and I will amend.\n\n## A. Layout\n\n1. **`shared/` contains seed data only — no `CREATE TABLE`.** All DDL lives in `postgres/` and `sqlite/`. (`migration-approach.md` §2, corrected under consistency-pass finding F28.)\n2. **`postgres/` serves PostgreSQL *and* CockroachDB.** No third directory. (Contract §9 of the out-of-scope list.)\n3. **Two goose invocations, separate version tables** (`goose_db_version_shared`, `goose_db_version_<driver>`). One shared table across two directories interleaves independent sequences and corrupts history. (`migration-approach.md` §2.)\n\n## B. Every constraint is named — this one is load-bearing\n\n4. **No anonymous constraints anywhere.** Error translation matches on SQLSTATE *plus the constraint name*, never on message text, because CockroachDB's message and `DETAIL` structure are not portable and its SQLSTATEs have a documented history of drift. An unnamed constraint is a sentinel that cannot be produced. (Contract §4; `research-cockroachdb-postgres-semantics.md` §3.)\n5. Names the contract already fixes: `uq_user_credential_username`, `fk_user_credential_method`, `ck_user_credential_secret_state`, `ck_user_profile_phone_e164`, `ck_deletion_log_reason`, and `ck_user_profile_{street_address,locality,region,postal_code}_nonempty`. (Contract §5.)\n\n## C. The three differences that already bit us in design\n\nThese are the defects the behavioural conformance suite exists to catch. Each was invisible when either implementation was read alone.\n\n6. **Collation is explicit on `user_profile.name` in both engines** — `COLLATE \"C\"` on Postgres/CockroachDB, `COLLATE BINARY` on SQLite — in the DDL, not only in `ORDER BY`. Without it, page 1 of a search returns different rows per backend: Postgres sorts by database collation, SQLite's default byte order puts every uppercase letter before every lowercase one. (Contract §6.3.)\n7. **The trigram index is an expression index on `LOWER(name)` with `gin_trgm_ops`, not on the bare column** — a GIN index on bare `name` is not used by a `LOWER(name)` predicate, so the accelerator would accelerate nothing. Postgres/CockroachDB only; **absent on SQLite, omitted rather than faked.** (Contract §5 Indexes, §6.3.)\n8. **`country` carries `CHECK (country IS NULL OR (country = upper(country) AND length(country) = 2))`** — normalization at write, not only at query. A stored `us` is silently invisible to a search for `US`. SQLite's `upper()` is ASCII-only, which is sufficient for alpha-2 and is written down so nobody assumes it generalizes. (Contract §6.5.)\n\n## D. Per-column checks\n\n9. **UUID primary keys on every backend** — server-generated on Postgres/CockroachDB, app-generated in Go before insert on SQLite. No per-backend PK strategy. (Contract §6.6.)\n10. **Absence is single-valued: NULL means absent, `''` is invalid.** All four address columns nullable with non-empty `CHECK`s; `name` `NOT NULL` with `length(trim(name)) > 0`. (Contract §5.)\n11. **`phone`** — regex `CHECK` on Postgres/CockroachDB, the `GLOB` form given in contract §5 on SQLite. Both named `ck_user_profile_phone_e164`. Exact-match only per finding F9; the B-tree index would support prefix, the contract does not offer it.\n12. **`secret_state`** — the table-level `CHECK` tying `secret_state` to `secret`/`hash_algo`/`hash_cost` must be present, or a row with a secret and no algorithm becomes representable. (Contract §5.)\n13. **Timestamps** — `TIMESTAMPTZ` on Postgres/CockroachDB, `TEXT` RFC3339 UTC on SQLite.\n14. **Booleans** — native on Postgres/CockroachDB, `INTEGER` 0/1 on SQLite, mapped at the DAO boundary.\n\n## E. Foreign keys and `deletion_log`\n\n15. **`user_credential.user_id` → `user_profile(id)` `ON DELETE CASCADE`**; `method_id` → `auth_method(id)` **RESTRICT**. The asymmetry is deliberate and documented: deleting a profile destroys its credentials, deleting a credential leaves the profile standing. (Contract §6.2.)\n16. **`deletion_log.profile_id` has NO foreign key** — the row it names is meant not to exist. (Contract §5.)\n17. **`deletion_log` carries no name, phone, address, username or secret column.** A conformance test asserts this; the DDL must not make it false. (Contract §6.11.)\n\n## F. Seed data\n\n18. **`auth_method` seeded with one `'password'` row, idempotent via `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT (name) DO NOTHING`.** In `shared/`, as a migration — not a runtime bootstrap. (`migration-approach.md` §4.)\n19. **New auth methods are inserted as data, never added as migrations.** A migration to add `passkey` means something upstream has been misunderstood — flag it. (Contract §6.7.)\n\n## What I will not do at review\n\nRe-open decisions. If the DDL is correct against this list and something still looks wrong to me, that is a **contract amendment** with a signed entry in the amendments log, not a review comment asking for a change the contract does not require. The contract is authoritative and the code follows it; where the code has found the contract wrong — which has happened three times, each time caught by the person implementing against it rather than by anyone reading it — the fix is to amend the document.\n\n---\n\n*Work-By: Priya Nandakumar (05 Data Ops lead, Claude Opus 5). Derived from existing rulings; no new decisions. Items 6–8 trace to defects found by Yusuf Karadag (DDL/nullability review) and the S2 adversarial pair with Anders Vogel; item 17 to Beatriz Achterberg's retention work; the F28 layout correction to the cross-track consistency pass.*\n"
}

Both rules noted. No `AskUserQuestion` to Warren for anything already on the record — questions go to Dana by message. I checked rather than waited: **no PRs are open yet and no DDL exists on main**, so there's nothing to review right now. LT-36/LT-37 are still in implementation. So I used the gap to publish `05-data-ops/ddl-review-checklist.md` — nineteen checks, each citing the contract, `migration-approach.md`, or `pii-governance.md` as its source, and explicitly introducing no new decisions. **A review checklist that only exists in the reviewer's head is a rewrite waiting to happen**, and this session has already shown that flagging something before implementation costs one message where finding it at review costs a rewrite (§1a). The four I expect to matter most: **Every constraint must be named** — this is the load-bearing one, not style. Error translation matches on SQLSTATE *plus constraint name*, never message text, because CockroachDB's message structure isn't portable. An unnamed constraint is a sentinel that cannot be produced. **Collation explicit in the DDL**, not only in `ORDER BY`, or page 1 of a search differs per backend. **The trigram index on `LOWER(name)`**, not the bare column, or the accelerator accelerates nothing. And **`shared/` is seed data only** — the F28 correction, which is the line most likely to be missed by anyone working from an older read of the migration doc. I also told Renata how I'll review: if the DDL is correct against the checklist and something still looks wrong to me, that's a **contract amendment with a signed log entry**, not a review comment demanding a change the contract doesn't require. The contract is authoritative; where the code has found it wrong — three times now, every time caught by whoever was implementing against it — the fix is to amend the document. === postgres/00001_initial_schema.sql ===
-- +goose Up
-- Postgres/CockroachDB DDL per 05-data-ops/multi-db-strategy.md §5.
-- Every constraint is named — error translation matches on SQLSTATE plus
-- constraint name, never message text (contract §4; ddl-review-checklist.md B).

CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pg_trgm;

CREATE TABLE auth_method (
	id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
	name TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
	requires_secret BOOLEAN NOT NULL,
	is_active BOOLEAN NOT NULL,
	created_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
);

CREATE TABLE user_profile (
	id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
	name TEXT COLLATE "C" NOT NULL CHECK (length(trim(name)) > 0),
	phone TEXT CONSTRAINT ck_user_profile_phone_e164 CHECK (phone IS NULL OR phone ~ '^\+[1-9][0-9]{1,14}$'),
	street_address TEXT CONSTRAINT ck_user_profile_street_address_nonempty CHECK (street_address IS NULL OR length(trim(street_address)) > 0),
	locality TEXT CONSTRAINT ck_user_profile_locality_nonempty CHECK (locality IS NULL OR length(trim(locality)) > 0),
	region TEXT CONSTRAINT ck_user_profile_region_nonempty CHECK (region IS NULL OR length(trim(region)) > 0),
	postal_code TEXT CONSTRAINT ck_user_profile_postal_code_nonempty CHECK (postal_code IS NULL OR length(trim(postal_code)) > 0),
	country TEXT CHECK (country IS NULL OR (country = upper(country) AND length(country) = 2)),
	source TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (source IN ('direct','idp_cache')),
	created_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
	updated_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
);

-- Expression index on LOWER(name), not the bare column — a GIN index on
-- bare name is not used by a LOWER(name) predicate (checklist item 7).
CREATE INDEX idx_user_profile_name_trgm ON user_profile USING GIN (LOWER(name) gin_trgm_ops);
CREATE INDEX idx_user_profile_phone ON user_profile (phone);

CREATE TABLE user_credential (
	id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
	user_id UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES user_profile(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
	username TEXT NOT NULL,
	method_id UUID NOT NULL CONSTRAINT fk_user_credential_method REFERENCES auth_method(id),
	secret BYTEA,
	secret_state TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (secret_state IN ('none','set','revoked')),
	hash_algo TEXT,
	hash_cost INTEGER,
	created_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
	updated_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
	CONSTRAINT ck_user_credential_secret_state CHECK (
		(secret_state = 'set'  AND secret IS NOT NULL AND hash_algo IS NOT NULL AND hash_cost IS NOT NULL)
		OR
		(secret_state IN ('none','revoked') AND secret IS NULL AND hash_algo IS NULL AND hash_cost IS NULL)
	)
);

CREATE UNIQUE INDEX uq_user_credential_username ON user_credential (LOWER(username));
CREATE INDEX idx_user_credential_user_id ON user_credential (user_id);
CREATE INDEX idx_user_credential_method_id ON user_credential (method_id);

CREATE TABLE deletion_log (
	id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(),
	profile_id UUID NOT NULL,
	source TEXT NOT NULL,
	reason TEXT NOT NULL CONSTRAINT ck_deletion_log_reason CHECK (reason IN ('retention_sweep','subject_request')),
	external_ref TEXT,
	job_run_id TEXT,
	deleted_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
);
-- No name, phone, address, username or secret column — contract §6.11,
-- checklist item 17.

-- +goose Down
DROP TABLE deletion_log;
DROP TABLE user_credential;
DROP TABLE user_profile;
DROP TABLE auth_method;

-- +goose Up
-- auth_method is a lookup table, not a native enum — new methods are
-- inserted as data, never a migration (contract §6.7, checklist item 19).
-- Idempotent so a re-run never duplicates the seed row (checklist item 18).
INSERT INTO auth_method (name, requires_secret, is_active)
VALUES ('password', true, true)
ON CONFLICT (name) DO NOTHING;

DELETE FROM auth_method WHERE name = 'password';

result
36:**Mechanism 04 needs:** goose applies one directory per invocation, so a migration run is **two invocations** — `shared/` then the backend directory — and each needs its own version table (`goose -table goose_db_version_shared`, then `-table goose_db_version_<driver>`). One shared table across two directories would interleave version numbers from independent sequences and corrupt the history. Both invocations must succeed for the run to count as successful.

-- collations — no trigram index (no SQLite equivalent, omitted rather than
-- faked, per the contract's dialect table).

PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;

CREATE TABLE auth_method (
	id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
	name TEXT NOT NULL UNIQUE,
	requires_secret INTEGER NOT NULL,
	is_active INTEGER NOT NULL,
	created_at TEXT NOT NULL
);

CREATE TABLE user_profile (
	id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
	name TEXT COLLATE BINARY NOT NULL CHECK (length(trim(name)) > 0),
	phone TEXT CONSTRAINT ck_user_profile_phone_e164 CHECK (phone IS NULL OR (phone GLOB '+[1-9]*' AND length(phone) BETWEEN 8 AND 16 AND NOT phone GLOB '*[^+0-9]*')),
	street_address TEXT CONSTRAINT ck_user_profile_street_address_nonempty CHECK (street_address IS NULL OR length(trim(street_address)) > 0),
	locality TEXT CONSTRAINT ck_user_profile_locality_nonempty CHECK (locality IS NULL OR length(trim(locality)) > 0),
	region TEXT CONSTRAINT ck_user_profile_region_nonempty CHECK (region IS NULL OR length(trim(region)) > 0),
	postal_code TEXT CONSTRAINT ck_user_profile_postal_code_nonempty CHECK (postal_code IS NULL OR length(trim(postal_code)) > 0),
	country TEXT CHECK (country IS NULL OR (country = upper(country) AND length(country) = 2)),
	source TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (source IN ('direct','idp_cache')),
	created_at TEXT NOT NULL,
	updated_at TEXT NOT NULL
);

CREATE INDEX idx_user_profile_phone ON user_profile (phone);
-- No trigram/expression index here — SQLite has no GIN equivalent;
-- Search falls back to a full scan, documented in the contract.

CREATE TABLE user_credential (
	id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
	user_id TEXT NOT NULL REFERENCES user_profile(id) ON DELETE CASCADE,
	username TEXT NOT NULL,
	method_id TEXT NOT NULL CONSTRAINT fk_user_credential_method REFERENCES auth_method(id),
	secret BLOB,
	secret_state TEXT NOT NULL CHECK (secret_state IN ('none','set','revoked')),
	hash_algo TEXT,
	hash_cost INTEGER,
	created_at TEXT NOT NULL,
	updated_at TEXT NOT NULL,
	CONSTRAINT ck_user_credential_secret_state CHECK (
		(secret_state = 'set'  AND secret IS NOT NULL AND hash_algo IS NOT NULL AND hash_cost IS NOT NULL)
		OR
		(secret_state IN ('none','revoked') AND secret IS NULL AND hash_algo IS NULL AND hash_cost IS NULL)
	)
);

CREATE UNIQUE INDEX uq_user_credential_username ON user_credential (LOWER(username));
CREATE INDEX idx_user_credential_user_id ON user_credential (user_id);
CREATE INDEX idx_user_credential_method_id ON user_credential (method_id);

CREATE TABLE deletion_log (
	id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
	profile_id TEXT NOT NULL,
	source TEXT NOT NULL,
	reason TEXT NOT NULL CONSTRAINT ck_deletion_log_reason CHECK (reason IN ('retention_sweep','subject_request')),
	external_ref TEXT,
	job_run_id TEXT,
	deleted_at TEXT NOT NULL
);

// New opens a connection against dsn (a file path, or ":memory:").
// Registered with internal/dao's factory in register.go. There is no
// retry wrapper here at all — not a no-op shim. SQLite has no
// retryable-serialization error class, so a no-op would be ceremony
// implying a shared abstraction that does not exist (contract §4).
func New(dsn string) (dao.Repository, error) {
	db, err := sql.Open("sqlite", dsn)
	if err != nil {
		return nil, fmt.Errorf("sqlite: opening connection: %w", err)
	}
	if _, err := db.Exec("PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON"); err != nil {
		return nil, fmt.Errorf("sqlite: enabling foreign_keys: %w", err)
	}
	// SQLite allows only one writer at a time; a single connection avoids
	// SQLITE_BUSY from this package's own concurrent use within a process.
	db.SetMaxOpenConns(1)
	return &repository{db: db}, nil
}

func (r *repository) Profiles() dao.ProfileRepository       { return profileRepo{r} }
func (r *repository) Credentials() dao.CredentialRepository { return credentialRepo{r} }
func (r *repository) Methods() dao.AuthMethodRepository     { return authMethodRepo{r} }

func (r *repository) Close() error { return r.db.Close() }

Found it. The cascade guarantee doesn't hold on SQLite the way my contract claims — amending, then reviewing.
Bash python3 - <<'PYEOF' import io p="multi-db-strategy.md"; s=io.open(p,encoding="utf-8").read() old=""
input
{
  "command": "python3 - <<'PYEOF'\nimport io\np=\"multi-db-strategy.md\"; s=io.open(p,encoding=\"utf-8\").read()\n\nold=\"\"\"*Enforcement: `ON DELETE CASCADE` on the FK; the asymmetry is stated in `pii-governance.md`.*\"\"\"\nassert s.count(old)==1\nnew=\"\"\"*Enforcement: `ON DELETE CASCADE` on the FK **plus, on SQLite, `foreign_keys` enabled on every connection** — see below; the asymmetry is stated in `pii-governance.md`.*\n\n   **The SQLite enforcement site is not the FK alone, and saying so was an error in this contract (amendment A3).** SQLite does not enforce foreign keys unless `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON` is set, and that pragma is **per-connection, not a property of the database file**. Three consequences, each of which fails *silently* — no error, just a cascade that does not happen:\n   - A `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON` statement inside a **migration file** does nothing useful: it applies to the migration runner's connection only, is not persisted, and is a no-op inside a transaction, which is how goose applies migrations by default.\n   - `db.Exec(\"PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON\")` on a `*sql.DB` sets it on **one pooled connection**, not on the pool. It holds only while that exact connection survives and is reused — so it is broken by a connection error causing the pool to reopen, by any later `SetConnMaxLifetime`/`SetConnMaxIdleTime`, or by raising `SetMaxOpenConns` above 1 for read concurrency. None of those changes look like they touch foreign keys.\n   - **The correct site is the DSN**, so every connection acquires it by construction — for `modernc.org/sqlite`, `_pragma=foreign_keys(1)`.\n\n   Why this matters beyond tidiness: with foreign keys off, deleting a profile leaves its credentials orphaned, `DeleteExpired`'s cascade silently retains credential rows past their profile's retention window, and `ErrInvalidMethod` can never be produced because the `method_id` FK does not fire. A governance control, a retention guarantee and an error sentinel all rest on this one pragma.\"\"\"\ns=s.replace(old,new)\n\nold2=\"\"\"- `deletion_log` carries no PII-shaped column (§6.11).\"\"\"\nassert s.count(old2)==1\nnew2=\"\"\"- `deletion_log` carries no PII-shaped column (§6.11);\n- **deleting a profile actually removes its credentials, asserted on every backend** — the cascade is a governance guarantee (§6.2) whose SQLite enforcement depends on a connection-level pragma that fails silently when absent, so it must be tested rather than assumed. Conversely, deleting a credential must leave its profile standing;\n- **an invalid `method_id` returns `ErrInvalidMethod` on every backend** — same reason: on SQLite the foreign key does not fire at all without that pragma, so a passing Postgres test proves nothing here.\"\"\"\ns=s.replace(old2,new2)\n\nold3=\"\"\"| A2 | 2026-09-12 |\"\"\"\nassert s.count(old3)==1\nnew3=\"\"\"| A3 | 2026-09-12 | §6.2, §7 | The SQLite enforcement site for the cascade guarantee is the FK **plus a connection-level `foreign_keys` pragma**, set in the DSN; §7 gains cascade and FK-violation assertions | §6.2 named `ON DELETE CASCADE` as the enforcement site, which is true on Postgres/CockroachDB and **false on SQLite**, where foreign keys are off unless enabled per connection. The failure is silent — no error, just a cascade that does not happen — and it would have taken a governance control (§6.2), a retention guarantee (`pii-governance.md`) and an error sentinel (`ErrInvalidMethod`) with it. §7 had no assertion covering any of the three, so no test would have caught it. Found reviewing LT-37 | Authored: **Priya Nandakumar**. Surfaced by: **Renata Cole**'s LT-37 SQLite migration and driver — the defect is in my contract's enforcement site, not in her code, which compensates correctly for today's configuration |\n| A2 | 2026-09-12 |\"\"\"\nio.open(p,\"w\",encoding=\"utf-8\").write(s.replace(old3,new3))\nprint(\"A3 added and signed\")\nPYEOF",
  "description": "Amend contract with A3 — SQLite FK enforcement site"
}

Constraint names match the Postgres migration, which is what makes cross-backend sentinel translation possible at all.\n\n**One real finding, and the fault is in my contract rather than in this code.**\n\n### `PRAGMA foreign_keys` — the cascade guarantee rests on a per-connection setting\n\nSQLite does not enforce foreign keys unless `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON`, and **that pragma is per-connection, not a property of the database file.** Three things follow, each failing *silently* — no error, just a cascade that does not happen:\n\n1. **The `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON;` at the top of the migration does nothing useful.** It applies to the migration runner's connection, is not persisted, and is a no-op inside a transaction — which is how goose applies migrations by default. Suggest deleting it, since its presence implies the schema enforces something it does not.\n\n2. **`db.Exec(\"PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON\")` in `New()` sets it on one pooled connection, not on the pool.** It works today because `SetMaxOpenConns(1)` follows it, so that connection is the only one — but the guarantee then rests on an implicit chain. It breaks silently if a connection error makes the pool reopen, if anyone later sets `ConnMaxLifetime`/`ConnMaxIdleTime`, or if `MaxOpenConns` is raised for read concurrency. None of those changes look like they touch foreign keys.\n\n   **Suggested fix: put it in the DSN**, so every connection acquires it by construction — for `modernc.org/sqlite`, `_pragma=foreign_keys(1)`. Keep `SetMaxOpenConns(1)`; your writer-serialization reason for it is independent and correct.\n\n3. **Why this is worth a PR round rather than a note.** With foreign keys off: deleting a profile leaves credentials orphaned (contract §6.2, a governance control); `DeleteExpired`'s cascade silently retains credential rows past their profile's retention window; and `ErrInvalidMethod` can never be produced, because the `method_id` FK never fires. A governance guarantee, a retention guarantee and an error sentinel all rest on this one pragma.\n\n### What I got wrong, and have fixed\n\nContract §6.2 named `ON DELETE CASCADE` as *the* enforcement site for the cascade. That is true on Postgres/CockroachDB and **false on SQLite.** Amendment **A3** corrects it: the SQLite site is the FK *plus* a connection-level pragma set in the DSN.\n\n§7 also had **no assertion covering any of the three consequences**, so no conformance test would have caught it. A3 adds two: that deleting a profile actually removes its credentials on every backend (and deleting a credential leaves the profile), and that an invalid `method_id` returns `ErrInvalidMethod` on every backend. A passing Postgres test proves nothing about either on SQLite.\n\nYour 24 tests passing is consistent with this — today's configuration is correct; it is the *durability* of the guarantee that is thin, and the missing conformance assertion is mine.\n\n### Also still open from PR #6\n\nThe six anonymous constraints appear here too (`auth_method.name UNIQUE`, `user_profile.name`/`country`/`source` CHECKs, `user_credential.user_id` FK, the `secret_state IN` CHECK). Sep 12, 7:41 PM PDTPriya Nandakumar
PR #8 reviewed. **The SQLite DDL is right** — collation, the `GLOB` phone constraint, no faked trigram index, app-generated PKs, `INTEGER` booleans, and constraint names matching the Postgres migration, which is what makes cross-backend sentinel translation work at all. One finding, and **it's my contract's fault rather than the code's.** SQLite doesn't enforce foreign keys unless `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON` — and that pragma is **per-connection, not a property of the database file.** Renata's `db.Exec("PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON")` followed by `SetMaxOpenConns(1)` works today, because that's the only connection. But the guarantee then rests on a chain that breaks *silently*: a connection error reopening the pool, anyone later setting `ConnMaxLifetime`, or raising `MaxOpenConns` for read concurrency. **None of those changes look like they touch foreign keys.** The fix is small — put it in the DSN so every connection acquires it by construction. The `PRAGMA` line in the migration file is separately inert: per-connection, unpersisted, and a no-op inside goose's transaction. Its only effect is implying the schema enforces something it doesn't. **Why it earns a round rather than a note:** with foreign keys off, three things fail at once and all silently — deleting a profile orphans its credentials (§6.2, a governance control), `DeleteExpired`'s cascade retains credential rows past their retention window, and `ErrInvalidMethod` can never be produced because the FK never fires. A governance guarantee, a retention guarantee and an error sentinel all resting on one pragma. **Amendment A3**, signed with Renata as surfacer. §6.2 named `ON DELETE CASCADE` as *the* enforcement site — true on two backends, false on the third. And §7 had **no assertion covering any of the three consequences**, so no conformance test would have caught it. A3 adds two. That second half is the part that matters. Her 24 tests passing is entirely consistent with the defect: today's configuration is correct, and what's thin is the durability of the guarantee plus a missing assertion — which was mine to specify. It's the same shape as the migration-order finding: the test proved *a* configuration works, not that the guarantee holds.

result
migration-approach.md:20:  postgres/   # all DDL for Postgres + CockroachDB, plus the trigram index
migration-approach.md:31:- **`postgres/`** carries the full DDL for `auth_method`, `user_profile`, `user_credential` and `deletion_log` in Postgres/CockroachDB types, plus the `pg_trgm` trigram expression index on `LOWER(name)`.
migration-approach.md:32:- **`sqlite/`** carries the same four tables in SQLite types and collations, and no trigram index — there is no SQLite equivalent and it is omitted rather than faked.
migration-approach.md:34:**Nothing in §3–§6 changes, and neither does 04's design**, exactly as the earlier text promised: the two-invocation mechanism, the separate version tables, R1–R5 and the seed-data rule are untouched. R3's worked example still holds and in fact reads better now — a service can have `shared/` current with the `pg_trgm` index unapplied, which is why fail-closed must check both version tables. (`pg_trgm` is a PostgreSQL extension that indexes three-character fragments of text so that substring searches like `LIKE '%smith%'` can use an index instead of scanning every row — it is what makes name search fast. CockroachDB has the same capability built in; SQLite has no equivalent, so the index is omitted there rather than faked.). That parity gap is documented and deliberate (`./multi-db-strategy.md`); SQLite is the local/dev/demo backend, not a production peer.
migration-approach.md:60:**R3. The service fails closed when the schema is behind — checking BOTH version tables.** §2 creates two independent version sequences (`shared/` and the backend directory), so "the applied version" is two numbers, not one. The check must compare both, and must know which backend directory applies to the engine it is connected to. Checking only `shared/` would let a service pass with the `pg_trgm` index unapplied — caught by an objection turn, and it would have been a passing check that proved nothing. On startup the service compares both applied versions against the versions it was built against and refuses to serve if either is behind, rather than serving traffic against a schema missing a column it will query. The requirement is mine; whether the expected version is embedded at build time or derived from the migration files shipped in the image is 03's and 04's call.
migration-approach.md:63:- *Migration runner*: DDL rights (`CREATE`/`ALTER`/`DROP`, index creation) plus, on PostgreSQL only, whatever `CREATE EXTENSION pg_trgm` requires — on managed Postgres this is usually an allow-listed extension, otherwise it needs elevated privileges or the platform pre-installing it. CockroachDB needs nothing here; trigram indexes are native. SQLite has no privilege model at all.
=== could-not-verify list ===
## Could not verify

Stated as gaps rather than filled with inference:

- Whether the `#42858` SQLSTATE inconsistency extends to ordinary INSERT-time FK *data* violations (`23503`) on current CockroachDB versions — the closed issue documents a DDL-time case only.
- The exact current-version message/`DETAIL` structure diff between the engines for `23505`/`23503` — specifically whether CockroachDB populates `Detail` and `ConstraintName` on `pgconn.PgError` identically to PostgreSQL. **This gap is the direct reason §3 mandates SQLSTATE-only matching**; the rule holds regardless of how the gap resolves, which is why it did not need resolving before writing the contract.
- Whether `crdbpgx.ExecuteTx` has behaviourally diverged from the `database/sql` variant `crdb.ExecuteTx`.

## What this changes downstream

Postgres/CockroachDB's id column is UUID-typed, so a malformed string causes a driver-level error (SQLSTATE 22P02, invalid_text_representation) before any row lookup happens — currently falls through my translateError's "unclassified" catch-all. SQLite's id column is TEXT, so the same malformed string is just a lookup that finds nothing, returning ErrNotFound cleanly. §3a's table only says Get returns ErrNotFound, and §4 says "every backend returns the same sentinel for the same condition" — but doesn't address what "the same condition" means when the two backends' column types make a malformed identifier a different kind of failure to begin with. My proposed reading: treat 22P02 (and any similar malformed-identifier condition) as ErrNotFound uniformly — a syntactically invalid identifier can never correspond to an existing row, so "not found" is a defensible, caller-uniform answer, and it means callers never have to pre-validate UUID format before calling Get. Alternative would be ErrInvalidArgument (malformed input, not a lookup miss) — also defensible, just a different framing (input validation vs. lookup result). Your call — which one should I implement? I'll add the SQLSTATE 22P02 mapping to translateError once you rule, and the conformance test asserting parity either way. </cross-session-message> This came from another Claude session — not typed by your user, but very likely working on their behalf. Postgres/CockroachDB type `id` as `UUID`, so the driver raises `22P02` (`invalid_text_representation`) *before any row lookup*; SQLite types it as `TEXT`, so the same string is an ordinary lookup that finds nothing and returns `ErrNotFound`. Found by LT-38's conformance suite — which is the suite doing precisely the job it was required for.\n\n**Ruling: a malformed identifier is `ErrInvalidArgument`, not `ErrNotFound`.** The alternative — treating it as \"not found\", on the reasoning that an invalid id can never name an existing row — is defensible in isolation but inconsistent with the rest of this contract, and consistency decides it. Every other malformed input here already maps to an argument error rather than to a data outcome: a non-empty `ID` passed to `Create`, a `Country` that is not two characters, an out-of-range `Limit` or `Offset`. Making a malformed UUID the single exception, where bad input is silently reinterpreted as a legitimate business answer, would be the one inconsistency a caller could not predict from the rest of the document.\n\nIt is also the more useful answer operationally: a caller sending malformed identifiers has a bug, and `ErrNotFound` hides it forever behind a plausible 404 while `ErrInvalidArgument` surfaces it on the first call. Note for `../02-ai-security-architecture/`: this distinction leaks nothing about existence, because it is decided purely by *syntax* before any lookup occurs — a well-formed id for a row that does not exist still returns `ErrNotFound`, so the two cases cannot be used to probe which identifiers are real.\n\n**Enforcement site — and this is the part that matters more than the ruling.** The validation happens **once, above the backends**, in shared DAO code, not as two per-backend error translations that must agree. Every method taking an `id` validates UUID syntax before touching a backend. Parity is then *by construction*: Postgres never reaches the query and never emits `22P02` on this path, and SQLite never silently misses. Two translation tables that must be kept in agreement is the failure mode this contract has spent three amendments removing; one validator above both is a single site that cannot drift.\n\n`22P02` is still mapped to `ErrInvalidArgument` in the backend translation as a backstop, for any path where a malformed identifier reaches SQL another way. That mapping is defence in depth, not the enforcement site.\n\n**Conformance assertion (§7):** a malformed identifier returns `ErrInvalidArgument` on every backend, and a well-formed identifier naming no row returns `ErrNotFound` on every backend. **Ruled A4: `ErrInvalidArgument`, not `ErrNotFound`** — and unblocked Renata immediately rather than making her wait on the review turn running in parallel. Her `ErrNotFound` reading was genuinely defensible: an invalid identifier can't name a row, so "not found" is true. What decided it was **consistency with my own contract** — every other malformed input already maps to an argument error rather than a data outcome (non-empty `ID` to `Create`, two-character `Country`, out-of-range `Limit`). Making a malformed UUID the single exception, where bad input is silently reinterpreted as a legitimate business answer, is the inconsistency a caller couldn't predict from reading the rest. **The part that changes her implementation matters more than the ruling itself.** I told her *not* to translate `22P02` as the primary mechanism — instead validate UUID syntax **once, in shared code above both backends**. Then parity is by construction: Postgres never reaches the query, SQLite never silently misses. Two per-backend translation tables that must agree is precisely the failure mode this contract has spent three amendments removing; one validator above both is a single enforcement site that cannot drift. The `22P02` mapping stays as a backstop. And **two conformance assertions, not one** — malformed → `ErrInvalidArgument`, *and* well-formed-but-absent → `ErrNotFound`. Assert only the first and you can't distinguish a correct implementation from one that returns `ErrInvalidArgument` for everything. Another Claude session sent a message: <cross-session-message from="uds:/tmp/cc-socks/13170.sock" from-name="03-engineering-delivery-b7" from-mode="prompting"> Running LT-38 against real CockroachDB (v23.2.0) surfaced a genuine problem: `COLLATE "C"` — required by contract §6.3/checklist item 6 on "Postgres/CockroachDB" — is not valid syntax on CockroachDB at all. `CREATE TABLE t (name TEXT COLLATE "C")` fails with "invalid locale C: language: tag is not well-formed" (SQLSTATE 42601). CockroachDB only accepts ICU-style locale tags (e.g. `en`), not Postgres's special "C"/"POSIX" byte-order pseudo-locales. Empirically tested the actual behavior this requirement exists to guarantee (byte-order sort, uppercase before lowercase, matching SQLite's BINARY): CockroachDB's DEFAULT (uncollated) TEXT comparison already produces exactly that order — I sorted ('alice','Bob','Aaron','bob') with no COLLATE clause and got Aaron, Bob, alice, bob, the same byte-order result "C" gives on Postgres. So CockroachDB doesn't need the clause to get the guarantee; it needs to not have it at all, since the syntax itself is invalid there. The problem: migrations/postgres/00001_initial_schema.sql is one shared file for both engines per the "one directory, no third" rule, but this specific clause can't be expressed as one piece of portable SQL — Postgres needs `COLLATE "C"` explicitly (its own default collation is locale-dependent, not guaranteed byte-order, so omitting the clause on Postgres would NOT be safe the way it is on CockroachDB). Options as I see them: (a) the migration file omits COLLATE entirely and Postgres's database/template is provisioned with LOCALE 'C' at creation time instead (moves the enforcement site from column DDL to database creation — a 04/infra concern, not just mine); (b) some CockroachDB-specific per-statement conditional I haven't found yet; (c) something else you'd prefer. This is blocking the CockroachDB conformance run entirely right now (migration fails to apply), so it's not cosmetic — nothing runs against real CockroachDB until this resolves. 79:- **`COLLATE "C"`** — tells the database to compare text by raw byte value rather than by language-aware alphabetical rules. Byte order sorts all uppercase before all lowercase; a locale-aware order does not. Picking one explicitly is what makes two different engines agree.
409:| `name` | `string` | `TEXT COLLATE "C"` | `TEXT COLLATE BINARY` | NOT NULL | `CHECK (length(trim(name)) > 0)`. Collation named explicitly — see §6.3. |
499:3. **Case, collation and folding.** `ORDER BY LOWER(name) COLLATE "C" ASC, id ASC`, fixed and not caller-configurable, with collation named in DDL as well — `COLLATE "C"` on Postgres, `BINARY` on SQLite. Without this, page 1 of a search returns *different rows* per backend, because Postgres sorts by database collation while SQLite's default byte order puts every uppercase letter before every lowercase one. Cost accepted and named: `C` is byte order, so non-English names sort in an order a human would call wrong — identical-across-backends beats locale-pretty for a system whose whole premise is one contract over three engines. *Enforcement: the DDL collation clause, the `ORDER BY` expression, and a conformance test asserting identical ordering across backends.*
--- item 9 ---
505:9. **The two-package split** (`postgres` serving Postgres + CockroachDB, `sqlite` separate) and the engine-aware retry seam inside the former. *Enforcement: package boundaries; `decisions/multi-db-abstraction.md`.*

Sep 12, 8:12 PM PDTPriya Nandakumar
**Ruled A5 and unblocked her** — migrations split per driver string, four directories, `postgres/` and `cockroachdb/` differing in exactly one clause with identical constraint names throughout. Her diagnosis was excellent and did the hard part: she established both halves empirically against live CockroachDB v23.2.0. That `COLLATE "C"` is invalid syntax there, *and* that CockroachDB's uncollated default already yields the required byte order, *and* that omitting the clause on PostgreSQL is **not** equivalently safe because its default collation is locale-dependent. That asymmetry is what the whole amendment turns on, and a docs-derived answer would have had me guessing at both ends. **I rejected her option (a)** — dropping the clause and provisioning the PostgreSQL database with `LOCALE 'C'`. It moves the guarantee off the schema into a provisioning step: invisible at review, unenforced by anything the repo contains, silently wrong on any database created without it. That's the exact enforcement-site failure this contract has spent five amendments removing. **The mistake underneath was mine, and it's a specific kind.** I had written "one `postgres` package serves both engines" and "one `postgres/` migration directory serves both engines" as if they were the same claim. They never were — the Go package is shared because *query construction and dialect* are shared; DDL collation is a different artifact entirely. Contract item 9 now says so explicitly, because my original wording invited the conflation. What this episode actually was: **§6.3 asserted a guarantee, named its enforcement site, and the named site was syntactically impossible on one of the three engines.** Five reviewers, three cold reads and a consistency pass all read that line and none could have caught it. The only thing that could was trying to apply it to a live database. That's the fifth concrete proof of the argument the per-driver ruling was made conditional on — and the §7 "identical ordering across backends" assertion, which has existed since S2 and has *never once run against CockroachDB* because the migration wouldn't apply, can finally execute.

Sep 12, 9:33 PM PDTPriya Nandakumar
All five faces are up — my overlay plus all four hires confirmed in HUD mode with their own portraits. But the more consequential outcome is that **Yusuf's unasked flag contained two real defects**, and I've ruled them as amendment A6. **The E.164 constraint pair accepted different sets, and always had.** Postgres `^\+[1-9][0-9]{1,14}$` allows 2–15 digits; the SQLite `GLOB` allows 7–15. So **`+1234` passes one constraint and fails the other** — a profile writable on one backend and rejected on the other from identical input. That was provable by arithmetic from my own document, needed no test, and survived every review because both constraints are correct when read separately. Nobody compared them. **`GetByUsername` had three lowercasers serving one uniqueness guarantee** — Go's Unicode-full `strings.ToLower`, PostgreSQL's locale-aware `lower()`, SQLite's ASCII-only one — against a single `UNIQUE (lower(username))` index. Now folded in SQL, so the expression that builds the index is the one that evaluates the lookup. The durable part is the rule he generalized from his own A4 objection, now in §4: **a validation step is only a parity guarantee if the set of inputs it accepts is written down, not named.** "Validates UUID syntax" and "accepts the canonical 36-character lowercase hyphenated form only" read like the same sentence and are not. He derived that from being wrong-footed once, then immediately found two more instances without being asked to look. That's the pattern this whole track has produced, at its sharpest: the defects weren't hiding in hard places. They were in pairs of statements that each read correctly alone. Six amendments now, and the reviewers who found them were reading *relationships* — between two constraints, between an index and a query, between a document and its own summary — which is exactly what no single-document review is scoped to do.

Sep 12, 9:39 PM PDTPriya Nandakumar
**PR #24 verified and approved; contract side is PR #25.** I checked against the contract rather than the description, and specifically looked for the A5/A6 interaction — which was the likely miss. A5 split the migrations three ways only hours earlier, so a phone fix applied to "the Postgres migration" would have left CockroachDB accepting a set the other two reject. **It landed in both.** All three directories now accept exactly `+` followed by 7–15 digits. The username fold is right too: `WHERE LOWER(username) = LOWER($1)` with the raw username passed, both `strings.ToLower` calls gone. The column-side expression still matches the index so it stays usable, and the third lowercaser is *eliminated* rather than merely made to agree. She also kept the case-insensitive-lookup assertion distinct from the existing duplicate-on-create test, which matters — collision-on-insert and lookup-by-different-case are different properties, and asserting only the first would leave the read path's fold untested. **What's worth keeping from this pair.** Neither defect needed a test. The E.164 divergence is arithmetic off the page: `{1,14}` is 2–15 digits, `BETWEEN 8 AND 16` is 7–15, and `+1234` sits in the gap. It survived a consistency pass, three cold reads, and every review this contract has had — because **both constraints are correct when read separately, and nobody read them as a pair.** That's the same shape as every other defect this track has produced. The artifacts were individually right; the relationship between them was wrong. What made these findable wasn't more careful reading — it was a rule about *where to look*: compare the two dialect expressions of a single requirement. Yusuf derived that rule from being wrong-footed once, then applied it without being asked. Six amendments, and the contract is stronger for every one of them.

None of them asked the prior question: *will this change actually apply to a database that already exists?* A corrected `CHECK` inside an already-applied `00001` is correct text and a **no-op migration** — goose never re-runs an applied version, so a live database keeps the old constraint forever while the file, the tests and the reviewer all look right.\n\n20. **Never edit an already-numbered migration that could plausibly have been applied anywhere** — any environment, any CI run, any developer's local database. Corrections ship as a **new numbered migration**, never as an edit to an old one. The only safe window for editing a migration in place is before it has ever been applied outside the branch that introduces it, and by the time it is in a PR that window is usually shut.\n21. **A change to a constraint is a `DROP` and `ADD` in a new migration**, applied to both `postgres/` and `cockroachdb/` (and `sqlite/` where relevant) — one file per directory, same version number, same constraint name.\n22. **Verify by incremental apply, not against a fresh schema.** A fresh-schema test passes for an edit to `00001` — which is precisely why this failure hides. The check that catches it is applying the *previous* state and then migrating forward, which is also what the deployed pipeline does. Same principle as the migration-order defect: **the thing tested and the thing run must be the same artifact.**\n23. **CockroachDB rejects a same-transaction `DROP` + `ADD` of a same-named constraint**, so such a migration needs `-- +goose NO TRANSACTION`. Nolan's correction was the right one and I took it: **three strikes on the same blind spot is a process gap, not a scorecard.** So instead of adding a fourth checklist entry, the principle is now stated *once*, authoritatively, in the contract's §7 — and `ddl-review-checklist.md` cites it rather than restating it, which is the restatement discipline this track has already been bitten by five times. > Verification exercises the sequence the live system executes, not an idealized construction of the end state. Four defects in this project were instances of it: an integration suite applying migrations in its own order, a SQLite harness setting its own pragma, a fresh-schema build standing in for an incremental apply, and CockroachDB's `NO TRANSACTION` requirement. In every case **the test was well written and tested the wrong artifact.** A suite that builds its own version of the thing under test cannot fail in the way the real thing fails. The fourth is the sharpest argument, and it's Nolan's point about the derived-context retry issue generalized: verifying correctly didn't merely catch the first defect — it *reached* a second one the other method had no access to. A fresh-schema apply never performs a `DROP`+`ADD`, so it could not have encountered that limitation no matter how carefully anyone read the code.
